Introduction
In the list, occurring in a number of the Puranasthe Agni or AgneyaMahapurana is accorded the eighth place. It is so named because it originated
as a composition in praise of Agni, a form of Visnu. It is attributed to Vyasa.
It is narrated as usual by his disciple Sita, and was received by him from the
sage Vasistha, to whom it was communicated by Agni. According to the
information preserved in the Agni-Purana itself, it contains fourteen thousand
verses. But the Bhagavata! and the Matsya Puranas? speak of Agni Purana
consisting of 15000 and 16000 verses respectively. In chapter 272 it states that
it contains twelve thousand verses and in its last chapter it claims to contain
fifteen thousand verses, though it actually possessed 11457. Thus it records two
traditions about the number of verses comprising it. It is apparent that the
text of the Agni-Purina underwent several additions. The Purana is divided
into 383 chapters treating a variety of subjects. The Agni-Purana claims to
be an epitome of all branches of knowledge, classified into para and apara. So
the compiler of the Purana intends to impart the essence of all the worldly
as wellas divine knowledge which may help the people to achieve success
in this life and salvation in the next.®
A list of fifty topics discussed in the body, occurs in the last chapter of
the Agni-Purana. They are (i) ten incarnations of Visnu, (ii) summary of the
Ramayana, (iii) Summary of the Mahabhdrata, (iv) Summary of the Harivamsa, (v) the Vaisnavagama, (vi) worship, (vii) initiation (diksa), (viii)
consecration of images, (ix) installation of images, (x) iconography, (xi)
architecture, (xii) spells and charms, (xiii) the Saivagama, (xix) Sakta worship,
(xv) Sun worship, (xvi) dissolution, (xvii) the description of consecration,
(xviii) geography, (xix) description of the sacred places such as Gaya, Gated
and Prayaga, (xx) astronomy, (xxi) astrology, (xxii) astrology and ritual]
conferring victory in war, (xxiii) various aeons of Manu, (xxiv) rules of caste
and stage. (xxv) impurity (a@Sauca), (xxvi) the duty of the king, (xxix) gifts,
(xxx) different vows (vratas), (xxxi) law code, (xxxii) propitiatory rituals
according to the four Vedas, (xxxiii) description of the solar dynasty, (xXxiv)
description of the lunar dynasty, (xxxv , military-science (dhanurveda), (xxxvi)
medicine (Ayurveda), (xxxvii) music, (xxxviii) polity (arthaSdstra), (xxxix)
mimdmsa philosophy, (xl) nydya philosophy, (xli) the various Puranas and
their praises (Mahatmya), (xlii) metrics, (xliii) grammar, (xliv) rhetoric,
(xlv) lexicography, (xlvi) phonetics, (Siksa), (xlvii) (Kalpa), (xlviii) dissolution, (xlix) Vedanta philosophy and (1) the eight-fold yoga. It says that
nothing can be richer in substance than this Purana.’
It deviates very materially from the definition given by its own author of

The Agni-Mahapurdana
the contents of a Purina. These Agni Purana declares to be five : primitive
creation ; subsequent creations ; the genealogies of demigods and Kings; the
aeons of Maaus and the histories of royal dynasties. These, however, occur
but imperfectly in the Agni-Purana, and the far greater portion of its contents
is of a widely different characters.°
The date of Agni Purana isa vexed problem. To arrive ata probable
date of its compilation, the date of interpolations made in the work must be
fixed. The fact that the Agni-Purana is included in almost all the lists of the
puranas ; mentioned by Alberuni in A.D. 1030; and attained an encyclopaedic character by the time of Vallabhasena; and was considered spurious by him
makes it earlier than 1100 A.D.® Pargiter’ holds that the Agni-Purdna was
written sometime after A.D. 900. According to Upadhyaya® since AgniPurana borrows much from the Kavyadarsa of Dandin and Bhoja is indebted
to Agni-Purana, so its date would fall between A.D. 700-900.
As Agni-Purana incorporated Vaisnava Paficaratra, the Gita etc. so it
became a Vaisnava Purana. It stresses the worship of Krsna, as Narayana or
Visnu. Inthe opening chapters Agni 1s represented as Vishnu, Kalagni and
Rudra. The same idea is repeated in chapter 174, where it is stated that
Visnu has two forms, one of which is Agni, praised in Agni-Purana. It
adds that Agni is but the manifestation of Visnu, and is meditated upon,
worshipped, remembered, praised and respected because he destroys all sins.
At the same time it included Saivagama, worship of Siva, as the Linga, obscene tantric rituals in honour of that form of deity ® On account of this the
vaisnavite Padma-Purana condemned the Agni-Purana as a tamasa Purana,
leading to hell.!° It was compiled, therefore, probably anterior to any wide
separation between the Saiva and Vaisnava sects, and it was certainly prior
to that modification of the Vaisnava faith, which pays such infinite veneration
to Krsna as Gopala or Govinda or Bala-Gopila, the cowherd or the infant
god, no allusion to whose worship has been found, nor has the name of his
favourite, mistress Radha, once been found.”
Of all the rivers the Ganga occupies the greatest attention of the AgniPurina. A whole chapter is devoted to it, and besides being extolled everywhere it is declared to be capable of destroying all sins only if people remember it. Gay&iis pronounced to be the best of all the holy places, it ranking
second to Ganga in two lists of holy spots and heading three long chapters
to the importance of Gaya and the procedure to be adopted for Gaya-Sraddha,
So though the place of first compilation of the Agni-Purina is doubtful, it
seems that final reduction took place somewhere in North East India, very
near to Ganga and the city of Gaya, which had attained prominence under
Palas. This place may be the banks of Narmada for it states that a bath in
the Ganga gives salvation which is gained by only a look at Narmada.*
Harprasad Shastri!® holds that the Agni-Purdna was compiled somewhere
in Bengal or Bihar far from Puskara, at a place difficult to reach. S.K. De"
argues that the available manuscripts of the Agni-Purana are mainly in

Introduction 7
Bengali or Devanagari scripts and the Bengali manuscripts are the largest and
So in his opinion it indicates an eastern origin of the Agni-Purana or at least
its popularity in this region. On the ground of its wide use, R.C. Hazrals
opines that the Agni-Purdna originated in eastern Orissa or in the WestBengal (most probably in the latter). Jackson}° regards the Agni-Purdna as
Gaya recension of the Purana and his view js supported by Rapson.!”? Thus
a controversy exists about the exact place of origin or compilation of the
Agni-Purana.
After the customary opening, the Agni-Purana describes in detail the ten
incarnations of lord Visnu. Chapters 2-4 deal with the Matsya, the Kirma
and the Varaha incarnations. The following seven chapters (5-11) dealing
with the incarnation of Visnu, forma Summary ofthe seven K4andas of the
Ramayana, each chapter standing for one Kanda. The story contained jn
them closely conforms to the original source i.e., the Ramayana.
The Agni-Purdna summarises the HarivathSa in one chapter (12) and out
of seventeen verses of the chapter 17 anda half verse are quoted verbatim
from the second chapter of the Harivamsa,
Next three chapters (13-15) of our texts compress the whole Story of the
Mahabharata. The origin of Kauravas and Pandavas is explai1.ed in the 13th
chapter. Chapter 14 treats the story of the Mahabharata and Pandavas, journey
to heaven, form the subject of the chapter 15, but this seems unnecessary and
spurious because with the description of the death of Pandavas the story
should have ended.
Chapter 16 of this Purana describes Buddha as the incarnation of Visnu,
The mention is made of the Buddhas and Arhatas as heretics (pasandins) and
of the mlecchas as kings, probably the Muslims.
Chapters 17-20 which describe the five essentials of a Purana are
borrowed from the Visnu, the Vayu, the Matsya and the Harivathéa Puranas.
Chapters 21-105,201 and 317-326 givea summary of the Vaisnava and Saiva
mantras (4gamas), Sakta and Sun worship. In chapters 21-70 Narada, Agni,
Hayagriva and Bhagavan are the interlocutors. These Chapters contain
description of worship, bathing procedure, building of Kunda, mantras related
to the four circles of Vasudeva, initiation, procedure, Mudrds, consecration.
sarvatobhadramandala, mandala procedure, sweeping procedure, samskiaras,
the Pavitra or purificatory thread, ceremonies of Homa, fruits of the erection
of temple etc , procuring of land, giving of Argha and Tila, architecture,
iconography, installation of images, the signs of Sudeva-idol, Pindika,
Salagrama etc., Matsya, Devi, Surya, Linga, twentyfour stotras of idol, the
measurement of Linga, the yoga connected with the protectors of the quarters,
attire, procedure of installation of Vasudeva, door, flag, Laksmi, Sudarsanacakra, erection of well, assembly hall, general rules of installation, repair work
of temple etc., setting out on a journey, and planting of trees.
Ai»,

The Agni-Mahapurdana
Thus we notice that these chapters are imbued with Paficaratra ideals and
prescribe the worship of Vasudeva, Sathkarsana, Pradyumna, Aniruddha, and
Narayana but word Paficaratra is nowhere mentioned in the Purana. The
chapters from 39-70 enumerate twentyfive Pajicaratra works and refer to the
Saptaratra sect. The Agni-Purana appears to summarise two of the HayaSirsa Paficaratra, the Adikanda and the Samkarsana-kanda. The Purana
dwells upon the iconography of sixtyfour yoginis, who are a type of tantric
goddesses. |
Chapters 71-106 relate to the worship of the Linga and several of the
tantric forms of Durga. The opening verse of this portion is a prayer of
Ganesa. It further lays down the worship of the deity and enumerates his
fourteen names. Here isvara (Siva) speaks to Skanda about the worship of
Siva, Sakti, Sun, the followers (gana) of Siva and other members of his family.
These chapters refer to Sivasarbhita, the Sarhhita mantra, mention the Samhita
trice, experts in Samhita. They are borrowed from Linga and the Saurakandas of the HayaSirsa Paficarétra. Mantras are abundantly used, as are
the acts and gesticulations with which they are muttered and recited. The
style in which they are narrated is, however, abrupt and obscure, and the
ceremonial so confusedly and indistinctly laid down that the whole has the
appearance of string of garbled extracts rather than a systematic detail.
There isa great similarity between these chapters with those of the SadradaTilaka and Mantra Mahodadhi. These chapters also contain a description
of the establishing of Agni, Canda-worship, Kapila worship, Pavitra, Damanaka, Samaya-initiation, Samskara initiation procedure, Nirvana-initiation,
the ractification of the art of installation, ekatattva, consecration etc., mantras
pertaining to procedure of installation, material for installation, installation
of Siva, Gauri, door, sun, palace, flag, repair, prasddalaksana, house-building,
town-planning.
The next chapter (107) is devoted to Sviyambhuva creation. This chapter
is followed by the Bhuvana-KoSa (the description of the universe) which
corresponds generally with the same in other Puranas. Eight chapters
(109-116) comprise the Tirthas or places of pilgrimage, of which however
it enumerates very few and those but briefly. These chapters are very important from the geographical point of view. It is noteworthy that Narmada and
Sri-Saila are especially noticed, while the northern mountains are not mentioned and also that Benaras (Vardnasi) is called Avimukta in its religious
character. The site of Benaras was the same as at present oF betw
Varna and Asi rivulets. Other pilgrim places mentioned by this Purana are
Puskara, Kuruksetra, Rajagrha and the rest. A separate chapter 1S
devoted to the praise of the Ganga, the Narmada, Prayaéga, and V4ranas!
each. A detailed description of Gaya is to be found in chapters 114-116. Its
importance is well brought out along with the procedure to be followed in
connection with journey to this holy place. The chapters relating to Gaya of
the Agni-Purana owe much to the Vayu-Purana.
een the

Introduction 9
Chapters 118-120 deal with the description of the Indian continent and
other parts of the world, India and other geographical places. They speak
of distances and dimensions of regions below and above it.
Chapters 121-149 dwell upon astrology, astronomy, palmistry, and
other branches of astrology leading to the king’s victory in the battlefield. In
chapters 121-123 the interlocutor Agni claims to abridge a treatise on astrology containing four lacs of verses. They discuss the auspicious and inauspicious asterism for perforating the ears of a child and also lay down auspicious time for taking betel-leaves. In chapters 124-149 Isvara speaks to Devi
about astrology, the science of gaining victory in war and various types of
spells, They enumerate the twelve rasis of the Sun, first mentioned in the
law code of Yajiavalkya. The order of the twentyseven asterisms begins
from ASvini to Revati which shows the astronomy of the third stage. The
astrological factors leading tothe rise and fall in the price of material
(arghakanda) and also the four circles (mandala) as Varahamihira does, but
this treatment is quite different from him. Chapter 130 of the Purana enumerates the four circles of asterisms, the Agneya, the Vayavya, the Varuna and
the Mahendra, with their results. These circles are connected with gaining
victory in war. According tothe Agni-Purana, Agneya circle leads to loss
of milk, flower and fruits, and a wholesale destruction of crops due to lack
of rain, eventually causing famine ; moreover it brings about the destruction
of the seven countries constituting the Uttarapatha, Saindhavas, Yamunas,
Gurjara, Bhoja, Bahlika, Jalamdhara, and Kasmira. The Vayavya circle
destroys the people of Dahala, Kamartpa, Kalinga, Koégala, Ayodhya, Avanti,
Konkana and Andhra. The Varuna circle brings about abundance of edibles,
cheap prices of land produce and a fierce fight among the kings. The
Mahendra circle leads tothe general happiness of people and a treaty of
peace among the warring kings. These chapters on astrology also betray a
deep tantric influence, for they describe the sixfold purpose, killing (marana),
stupefying (mohana), maddening (uccitana) bringing others under control
(Vasikarana) controlling the function of others (Vidravana) and the six types
of tantras such as pallava, yoga, rodhaka, samputa, prathama, and vidarbha.
The tantric goddesses like Kubjika, Bhelkhi, Tara, Tavarita, Candaghanta,
Karali, Sumukhi, Durmukhi, Ajita, Aparajita etc. are described together with
obscene and horrifying rituals.
From these, the Agni-Purana proceeds rather abruptly to the periods
of the Manvantaras (Ch. 130) and then to the civil institutes ot the Varna, a3
birth, investiture, marriage, conduct, death etc., the duties of religious
orders and varnas, the contemplation of the deity, conformably to the tenets
of the Vedanta, impurity and purification of things (chs. 150-167).
The next subject discussed is that of expiations for various types of sins,
A long string of Vratas or religious obligations, both special and occasional follow (chs. 75-207). The portion opens with a definition of the vow
Ma

The Agni-Mahdpurdna 10
and goes on to describe vows like Pratipada, Dvitiya, etc. Ekadasi, Dvadasi,
Trayodasi, Caturdasi, Sivaratri, ASoka-purnima, vara-vratas, Nakeatra,
Divasa, Masa, Dipadana, Masopavasa, Bhismapaficaka, Kaumuda étc. Besides
these vratas, chapters 201 and 203 describe nine circles (NavavyUharcana)
and the nature of hell (Narakasvaripam) respectively. |
Chapters from 208-217 deal with the subject of gifts as religious duties,
close with the description of corporeal austerities of a meritorious and pious
complexion.
The statecraft forms the subject matter of chapters 218-252 of the AgniPurana. The Purdna treats at considerable Jength, and with many interesting particulars, of the duties of princes, beginning with the ceremonies of
their coronation, and comprehending their civil and military obligations,
The description of state and kingship includes, seven limbs of a state, divinity
of the king, his qualifications, daily routine, duties and functions, royal
privileges. Under administrative organisation comes enumeration of qualifications and duties and functions of the ministers and other officers. A
description of civil administration contains appointing of chief minister, the
royal priest, the royal astrologer, the commander-in-chief, the royal chamberlain, ambassadors, betel-bearer, bodyguards, charioteer, superintendent of
royal kitchen, members of jury, scribes, gate-keepers, superintendent of
treasury, of elephants, horses, ports, royal engineers, teacher for arms and
weapons, heads of harem and magazines.
The fourfold expedients (Sima, Dana, Wanda, Bheda) and six measures
of royal policy (Sandhi, Vigraha, Yana, Asana, Dvaidhibhava, SamSaya are
also discussed. The forts played important part in the defence scheme of
ancient times, The Agni-Purdna discusses them in a separate chapter. It
dwells at length upon the march of the army (Ch. 228). The prognostication
of dreams, omens and portents are taken into account in chapters 229-32.
Besides these topics, this portion of the Purana deals with Sristotra, polity as
told by Rama, male and female marks, science of jewels etc.
In chapters 249-252, the Agni-Puraina treats the science of archery
(dhanurveda). It describes various types of offensive weapons and methods
to use them. The weapons are classified under five heads : weapons thrown
by some machines, those thrown by bands, those which are never thrown, but
held back, and natural weapons such as fist. The Purana gives much space
to the manufacture of bows, arrows, Strings etc. and deals extensively
with the ways and methods of training in archery.
Chapters (254-258) on the subject of judicature and law are literally
the same as the text of the Mitaéksara, a commentary on Yajnavalkya Sonrti.
Adjudication of civil and criminal cases is to be considered as important duty
of the king. It praises the rod of justice and calls punishment as danda. The
dana is of two types, one for internal and the other for external application.

Introduction
Seventyone crimes and their punishments are described in the Agni-Purana’s
chapters on Rajadharma.
The vyavahara, according to Agni-Purana enables us to know what is
just and what is unjust. In other words let us have an idea of the legality
of any action. A legal suit is defined as an action contrary to the rules of
gmrtis and good conduct and brought before the court by persons other than
the king and his officers. The chapterson Vyavahara take into account the
sources of law, eighteen branches of litigation, judge, his qualifications, assistants to the judicial bench, setting of court, assessors, evidences, witnesses,
documents, ordeals, oath, various crimes, punishments, judicial fines. Thus
these chapters give the definition of law, its sources, various titles of litigation, procedural Jaw, the law of evidence, matters of litigation and punishments. Besides these topics, the laws of inheritance, the boundary disputes,
and use of harsh language are treated in separate chapters (256-58'.
The chapters on law are followed by rather miscellaneous series regard:
ing the perusal of the Vedas, the averting of ill fortune, burnt offerings and
the worship of various deities viz., VaiSvadeva, deities of quarters, Vinayaka,
Mahesvara, Visnu and a chapter on the branches of the Vedas (chs.
The next chapter (272) on gifts to be made, when Puranas are read,
contains list of the Puranas and the enumeration of the number of stanzas
they contain.
The list of the Puranas is followed by genealogical chapters (273-278)
detailing the families of the Sun and the Moon, but more particularly the
latter and especially, the house of Yadu, and Puru to the time of Krsna and
the Pandavas. These chapters agree with the dynasties usually detailed but
the lists are forthe greater part dry and abrupt, while few of the ordinary
legends are preserved, those so concisely asto be very obscure. There are
some details relating to Krsna.
The next subject is medicine, taken from the instruction given by ~ Dhanvantari to Suéruta or from the medical work attributed to the latter. Chapters
279-300 and 369-370 discuss the treatment of not only human beings but
also of the elephant, the horse, the cow and even of trees. They describe the
various types of snakes and suggest treatment of their bite as well as that of
some injurious insects and animals. They again prescribe medicine against
diseases caused by malevolent elements and planets. The chapters on the
treatment of animals and trees are borrowed verbatim from the Visnudharmottara. Though the Purana claims to have borrowed Susruta’s idea
yet there is hardly anything in it pertaining to surgery. It only states that
a sore is to be opened with the help of a needle. The exposition of surgery
by the Agni-Purdna is very scanty. The classification of human diseases
under four heads, physical (S4ririka) mental (manasika) accidental (agantuka)
and natural (sahaja), the grouping of six rasas under two, saumya and
agneya ; and the description of cold and hot medicants (Sita and usna_ virya)

The Agni-Mahapurdna 12
are adopted from Susruta. The Purana declares that human body is made of
sixteen kandaras, sixteen jalas, six kurchs, four mamsarajjus, seven sivanyas,
nine hundred nerves (snayu), two hundred and ten joints (sandhi),
five hundred pesis contained in a female body. The human anatomy,
embryology and osteology are discussed separately in chapters 369-370. In
the field of osteology the Purana follows the view of Caraka and holds that
the human body consists of three hundred and sixty bones. Mercury is
known to the Purana but is not recommended as medicine. The part
(chs. 296-300) of Purana likewise includes much mystic medicine or curing
by charms. | ,
Another set of chapters on mystic rites and formulae and different forms
of Visnu, Siva and Devi follows. Chapters 301-316 contain worship of Surya,
various mantras, worship of Angdaksara, Paficaksara-worship, names of
Visnu, mantras of Narasithha etc., Trailokyamohana mantras, worship,
mantras and vidya of Tvarita, stupefying mantras and other spells.
It may be noted that in chapters 312-326 Isvara speaks to Skanda about
the various spells, the worship of Siva’s ganas, VagiSvari, Aghora, PaSupata,
Rudra and Gauri. :
The Mahatmya of the temple forms the subject matter of chapter 327.
The Agni-Purana contains eight chapters (328-335) on metrics. They
summarise the Pingala sUtras together with an unknown commentary on it.
' Metres dealt with are Gayatri, Usnik, Jagati, Tristubha etc. The metres are
classified as dissimilar, half similar and similar. -
A lone chapter (Ch. 336) discussing Vedic phonetics is rudimentary and
an abstract of the Naradiya-Siksa.
Poetry and rhetoric form the next subjects (chs. 337-338) and conform
to the system usually received. These chapters deal with definitions of poetry,
poetic talent, forms of poetry, alamhkaras, the definition of prose and its
varieties viz., clrnaka, uikalika, gandhivrta: fivefold Prose works like
Akhyayika. katha, Khanda-katha, Pari-kathd, Kathanika : difference between
Katha and Akhydyika, Kulaka, Muktaka, Kosa, Mahdkdvya, Kausiki vrtti
etc.
The Agni-Purana (ch 338) enumerates twentyseven types of Sanskrit
dramas viz., Nataka, Prakarana, Thamrga, Prahasana, Vyayoga, Bhiina,
Vithi, Anka, Trotaka, Natika, Sattaka, Silpaka, Karna, Durmalika, Prasthana, Bhanika, Bhani etc. It is probably the first text to catalogue so many
types of drama but it does not classify them into major and minor groups,
rupaka and uparipaka. It counts sattaka asa form of drama, first defined
by Rajasekhara whose Karptramaiijari is the earliest specimen Technical
terms like Pirvaranga, Nandi, Sttradhara, Vidusaka Amukha, five sandhis,
five arthaprakrtis etc, are explained in these chapters.
The next chapters (339-40) deal with sentiments like Srfigira etc., Riti,
concert hall, acting etc. The Agni Purana states the doctrine of Srigara
rasa as Ananda, alamkara and :asa. It enumerates four types of ritis, the

Introduction 13
function which closely follows the idea of Bhoja.
In chapters 343-45 Agni-Purana classifies the alathkaras into three categories, those of sound (Sabdaja), those of sense (artha) and those of both (Sabdartha) and most probably uses Slesa when it states that one who knows the
three types of alamk4ra and their analysis, and can embellish speech with
them, is called an expertin Sabdalathkaras and the Kavyamimamsa. The
definitions of rupaka, utpreksda, viSesokti, vibhdvand, apahnuti and samadhi
given by Agni-Purana are the same as those of Kavyadarsa of Dandin.
Merits and demerits of compositions are discussed in chapters 346-347.
Further, the definitions of 4ksepa, aprastutaprasatbsa, sama@sokti and pary4yokta are almost similarto those in Bhamaha. The Purana speaks of four
pakas of poetic composition but actually names three—the mIrdvikapaka, the
narikelapaka, and the ambupaka and defines only first Slesa, Prasada,
Madhurya, Sukuméarata etc. described as merits.
The following eleven chapters (349-359) treat with the rules of grammar
which is an abstract of the Candra grammar. This is evideat because according to Agni-Purana one who Knows and studies the Candra grammar is a
candraka. These chapters discuss rules of Sandhi, Subanta, formations of
Strilitga words and neuter words, K§arakas, Samisa, Taddhita, Unadi,
Tinanta and Krt.
Chapters 360-367 of the Agni-Purana deal with lexicon on Avyaya,
Nanartha, Bhimi-vana-Ausadhi, Nrbrahmaksatriya, Brahma, Ksatra-vitaSidra on the pattern of Amarasimha’s Amarakosa. The last chapter of this
lays down general names with their genders.
Chapetrs 369-370 give information about human anatomy.
The next chapter (371) describes hell and its various forms. After this,
chapters 372-376 treat yoga in its two varieties i.e., Raja-yoga and Hatha-yoga.
The Agni-Purdna says that a human body consists of seventytwo nerves
and the navel is their centre and the root (xanda) lies below it, wherefrom
they shoot out tothe different parts of the body. It also speaks of Kundalini and states that it is permeated with the essence of sup, moon and fire.
Chapter 373 deals with injunction that the Yogi should meditate upon the
Supreme after sitting in postures like the lotus (Padma), svastika, ardhacandra, (crescent), vira (brave), yoga, prasadhita, paryanka, and one liked by the
yogi himself. It also includes yama and niyama within the scope of yoga
which is not done by the Goraksapaddhati.
The knowledge of Brahma and philosophy of Vedanta form the subject
matter of the chapters 377-380. Last three chapters (381-383) present the
gist of Gita, Yama-Gita and the Mahatmya of the Agni-Purana.
On the final analysis, the Agni-Purdna shows that it owes much to the
various branches of literature of early medieval times and is especially indebted to the four Vedas, Upanisads, Smrttis, Visnudharmottara and the HarivamSa.
Upapuranas; the Visnu and the Matsya Puranas, the two epics, the Ramayana
Mai,

a
The Agni-Mahapurdna
and the Mahabharata; the Visnurahasya, the HayaSsirsa, the Gita, ihe
Sarhkhya, the Vedanta, the Sarhhita of Caraka and Susruta and Vrndamidhava, Naradiyasiks4 monosyllabic lexicography of Ksapanaka, the Pifgalasutras, the lexicon of Amarsithha, Bharata, Bhimaha, Dandin etc. Vaisnava
and Saivagamas. Thus Agni-Purana presents essence of all branches of
knowledge and is an encyclopedia of all that existed : Agneye hi puranesmin
sarvah vidyah pradarsitah (Agni-P. 383:51).
The Agni-Purana has come down to us in the following popular
editions :
(i) A critical edition of the Agni-Purana was published by Anandasrama
Series, Poona, 1900. It was edited by Hari Narain Apte.
(ii) Another edition was edited by R. Mitra in Bibliotheca Indica,
Caleutta, 1873-79.
(iii) Still another edition was published by Venkteswar Press, Bombay
from which the present edition has been published.
An English translation of this Purana was published by M. N. Dutta in
two volumes from Calcutta in the years 1903-1904. It is also noteworthy that
a handwritten manuscript of the Agni-Purdna named Vahni-Purana was
discovered by Dr. R.C. Hazra. He considers the Vahni-Purana as the
genuine Agni-Purana.
Modern works on ancient Indian polity, geography, sociology, civilization |
and culture profusely utilize the material available in the Agni-Purana. ‘Polity
in the Agni-Purana’, by Dr. Bambahadur Mishra; ‘Agni-Purdna ka KavyaSastriya Bhaga’, by Dr. Ram Lal Verma, ‘Agni-Purdnasya Visayanukramant’
by Dr. Ramashankar Bhattacharya, are some of the valuable works exclusively
based on the Agni-Purana.
DR. R.N. SHARMA,
Reader in Sanskrit,
Hindu College, University
